Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi doctor. I'm here because I feel like I'm constantly 'on the go' and just can't seem to slow down. It honestly feels like I'm being driven by a motor all the time, and it's becoming really exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually euphoric or irritable for several days in a row and needed much less sleep than usual? I don't have periods where I feel unusually euphoric or irritable, and my need for sleep stays pretty much the same. It's more of a constant, everyday restlessness rather than sudden phases.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often have trouble paying attention, staying organized, or finishing tasks, even when you’re not feeling especially worried? Or is the restlessness mostly happening because your mind is filled with worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely have a lot of trouble paying attention, often making careless mistakes and struggling to listen when people speak directly to me. It isn't because I'm worried or anxious, though; this inattention and my constant restlessness are just how I always function.\n\nDoctor: Besides feeling constantly on the go, do you often fidget, have trouble staying seated, interrupt people, or act before thinking? It has really been a long-standing issue for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: As a child or now, did you often talk excessively, have trouble waiting your turn, or blurt out answers before questions were finished?"

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi doctor. I'm here because I feel like I'm constantly 'on the go' and just can't seem to slow down. It honestly feels like I'm being driven by a motor all the time, and it's becoming really exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually euphoric or irritable for several days in a row and needed much less sleep than usual? During those times, did you also feel more impulsive, talkative, or overly confident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced anything like that. I don't have periods where I feel unusually euphoric or irritable, and my need for sleep stays pretty much the same. It's more of a constant, everyday restlessness rather than sudden phases.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often have trouble paying attention, staying organized, or finishing tasks, even when you’re not feeling especially worried? Or is the restlessness mostly happening because your mind is filled with worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely have a lot of trouble paying attention, often making careless mistakes and struggling to listen when people speak directly to me. It isn't because I'm worried or anxious, though; this inattention and my constant restlessness are just how I always function.\n\nDoctor: Besides feeling constantly on the go, do you often fidget, have trouble staying seated, interrupt people, or act before thinking? Which of those happens most often for you?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I don't really experience things like fidgeting, having trouble staying seated, or interrupting others very much. I'm not really sure about those specific behaviors, as it's more just that constant, overall drive to keep moving.\n\nDoctor: Did these attention problems and restlessness start back in childhood, before age 12, and were they noticeable at school or at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, these struggles definitely started during my childhood, well before I turned 12. I remember my teachers and parents always pointing out how restless I was and how I struggled to pay attention both at school and at home. It has really been a long-standing issue for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: As a child or now, did you often talk excessively, have trouble waiting your turn, or blurt out answers before questions were finished?\n\nPatient: I definitely talk excessively, and that's something I've done ever since I was a kid. I'm not as sure about having trouble waiting my turn or blurting out answers, but the constant talking is definitely a major thing for me.\n\nDoctor: As a child or now, did you often feel internally restless, like you couldn’t relax or had to keep yourself busy even when sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have always felt that way, both now and as a child before age 12. I constantly feel \"on the go\" as if driven by a motor, making it extremely difficult to just sit still and relax.\n\nDoctor: As a child or now, did you often leave your seat when you were expected to stay seated, like in class or meetings?"

Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.
